**Step 1:** The autopsy report (Evidence 2) states that Doug died from a fatal electric shock. Phoenix’s testimony (Testimony 3) claims that after shoving Doug, “he fell down on top of his cheap umbrella,” implying the death resulted from the fall or the impact with the umbrella.

**Step 3:** –  

**Step 4:** The contradiction is identified by comparing the *cause of death* indicated in the autopsy (electric shock) with the *mechanism of death* implied by Phoenix’s testimony (a fall onto an umbrella). Since an electric shock cannot be caused merely by falling onto an umbrella, the two statements cannot both be true.

{"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

**Step 2:** The generated spatial rules include Rule 4: *If XXX is behind HHH then HHH is in front of XXX.* This rule captures the relationship between “back” and “front” positions.

**Step 3:** Applying Rule 4, the claim in Evidence 6 (“blow to the back of the head”) implies that Atmey’s head is in front of the blow. Conversely, Testimony 5 (“blow landed upon my third eye”) implies the blow is in front of Atmey’s head. Since a single blow cannot simultaneously be both behind and in front of the same object, the rule highlights the inconsistency.

**Step 4:** Even without the rule, the spatial reasoning is straightforward: “back of the head” and “third eye (forehead)” are opposite sides of the head, so they cannot describe the same impact point.

Testimony 4: "The victim turned around and tried to run away, but..." Testimony 6: "She only got about 10 yards before she was stabbed in the back."

Evidence 6 (Witness's Photo) description: "In the photo, the victim wearing a scarf and a hat is standing to the right, facing left. The victim was standing close to the broken end. A man in jail uniform and chains is facing the victim standing to the left."

Also stabbing in back would be consistent with victim turned away. But photo shows victim facing attacker, not turned away. So contradiction between Evidence 6 and Testimony 4/6.

So victim is to the right of man, man is to the left of victim. That matches rule 2. Also victim facing left means she is oriented towards man. Testimony says she turned around (i.e., facing opposite direction). So contradiction arises because spatial relation of facing direction opposite.

Rule 5 about behind/in front not relevant. Rule 1 north/south not relevant. Rule 3 above not relevant. Rule 4 inside not relevant.

Thus we can cite Rule 2 to show spatial relation: victim is to the right of attacker, so attacker is to left of victim. Testimony says victim turned around (implies she would be to left of attacker? Actually if she turned away, she would be facing opposite direction, but still location same). The contradiction is about facing direction, not left/right. But we can still use rule 2 to assert positions.

Thus answer: evidence 6, testimony 4.

**Step 1:**  
Testimony 2 (by Lang) states that the victim was shot “from on high,” implying the shooter was positioned **above** the victim.  
Evidence 6 (Mr. Deacon’s Murder Notes) describes the shooting as “Shot from ABD through shoulder,” i.e., the bullet entered the victim’s lower right abdomen and exited the right shoulder, indicating the shooter was **behind** the victim, not above.

**Step 2:**  
The generated spatial rules include Rule 1 (above ↔ below) and Rule 5 (behind ↔ in front). These can be applied to compare the two described positions.

**Step 3:**  
- Using Rule 1, if the shooter were “above” the victim (as the testimony claims), the victim would be “below” the shooter.  
- Using Rule 5, if the shooter were “behind” the victim (as the evidence states), the victim would be “in front of” the shooter.  

A single shooter cannot simultaneously be both “above” and “behind” the same victim without additional qualifiers, and the two statements give mutually exclusive primary spatial relations. Hence, the rules highlight the inconsistency.

**Step 4:**  
Even without the explicit rules, the contradiction is evident: one source places the shooter high above the victim, while the other places the shooter directly behind the victim, describing the bullet’s trajectory from abdomen to shoulder. These two spatial descriptions cannot both be true for the same shooting event.

**Conclusion:** The contradiction is between **Evidence 6** and **Testimony 2**.
